It keeps a tier of slots in the card's memory - the room it has left, 85% of its memory less what Janas took and 1 GiB for the rest (`JANAS_GPU_TIER_MB=<n>` asks for n MiB), in pieces of 1 GiB that any driver allocates - and between two layers, where the CPU already sorts the router's choice, each chosen expert is either in the tier, or read into it in place of the one used least among sixteen (never one this layer counts on), or computed by the CPU from the layer's normalized input into its own rows, which the GPU then sums with the others in the router's order (`janas_m_expert_rows`, the CPU's expert kernels). Only the experts not in the tier are asked of the cache, so a tier hit never reads the disk. The arena is not copied to such a card any more, so the tier has its memory (before, a cache holding every expert was copied whole, and a copy that did not fit could leave pieces behind). `JANAS_GPU_TIER=0` turns the tier off. Checked as a card of its own on the development laptop's Arc, functional runs only: Qwen3-30B-A3B UD-Q3_K_XL with a tier of 1 GiB (480 of its 6,144 experts) and of 300 MB (140, a slot given up at nearly every layer) - sensible text, every pass whole on the GPU, no error under the validation layer with synchronization checks. The text is not the same to the bit with every tier: the two tiers' first 15 tokens are the same, then they part, as the run with drafts parts from the one without after 8-9 tokens - an expert computed by the CPU or by the GPU rounds differently (the GPU's expert products sum in their own order), and which computes it depends on what the tier holds.
- **Every fma() of the shaders one operation, as on the CPU: a mixture's tier no longer changes its answer**: glslang decorates a `precise` variable's multiplies and adds NoContraction but not its `fma()` calls, and GLSL.std.450's Fma without it may be done as a multiply and an add, two roundings - the Arc's driver did split some: the experts' activation (`expert_act.comp`, its e^x and reciprocal) came out a unit in the last place off the CPU's in about 1.5% of its values, which moved a block's scale in one expert in eighty. The shaders' SPIR-V now goes through `tools/spv_fma.c`, which decorates every Fma (built by `build.sh` with the build machine's compiler, `HOSTCC`, and writing the C array that `--vn` wrote). With that, and the CPU's experts quantizing the GPU's input with the same deterministic operations (`janas_q8k_quantize_det`), an expert computed by the CPU gives the GPU's bits: Qwen3-30B-A3B's logits on 160 tokens are the same to the bit with a tier of 300 MB, 1 GiB and every expert. Found with a probe that recomputed each GPU expert on the CPU (input, gate and up the same to the bit, the activation not). Every block's experts now go through the matvecs by default (`JANAS_GPU_MVID=16` as before: a prompt's blocks in `*_mmid`'s tiles), Q6_K_P's too (`q6kp_matvec.comp` with `ID`, its planes and level from the table): a prompt's GPU experts went through tiles that are not the CPU's bits and its other experts through the CPU, so the tokens with drafts and without parted after some ten (Qwen3-30B-A3B with a tier of 300 MB at the 11th, Qwen3.6-35B-A3B with one of 1 GiB at the 40th); now they are the same, 48 of 48 on both. The prompt as a card of its own on the laptop's Arc: 7% faster with every expert in the tier, 3% slower with 300 MB.
- **A mixture's whole pass on a card of its own 2.2 times as fast on a single token**: measured as a card of its own on the laptop's Arc (Qwen3-30B-A3B UD-Q3_K_XL, every expert in the tier, 160 tokens of fixed text one at a time, the second half): from 4.7 to 10.5 tokens/s; with a tier of 300 MB, the other experts on the CPU, from 3.1 to 8.5; Qwen3.6-35B-A3B Q4_K_M with a tier of 1 GiB 7.0 tokens/s. Four causes, found with the GPU's own timestamps: (1) between two layers the CPU's plan was read and written in six or seven copies of their own, each a submission waited for - now the choice of experts (and, with a tier, the experts' input) goes to the mirror at the end of a layer's commands and the tables come back at the start of the next one's, and the experts read into the tier and the CPU's rows go in one submission together (`janas_g_copies`); (2) the router's product went through `dense_mm`'s tiles of 64 rows by 64 vectors, three workgroups on a token - now `dense_mv.comp`, a workgroup a row, on up to 8 vectors (also the DeltaNet's beta and alpha, and qwen4exp's mixes, these not tried: the whole pass refuses them on a card of its own); (3) `moe_topk.comp` had one invocation a token search 128 logits nine times - now a workgroup a token, each search a reduction; the router from 1.07 to 0.11 ms a layer; (4) the experts' products on a few vectors went through `*_mmid`'s tiles of 16 vectors, nearly empty on a token (9 GB/s) - now through the matvec shaders of Q3_K, Q4_K and Q6_K with a constant `ID` that reads the weights, inputs and outputs from the same table (blocks of 16 tokens or fewer, `JANAS_GPU_MVID=<n>`, 0 never): gate and up from 55 to 26 ms a token. Every step checked: logits the same to the bit before and after the copies were moved; the same agreement with the CPU (145-150 of 160 tokens' most likely token, as Qwen3-4B's whole pass, 154); no error under the validation layer with synchronization checks; prompts as fast or faster (512 tokens 111.9 to 112.9 tokens/s, 120.7 with every block through the matvecs). With a tier holding every expert the tokens are the same with drafts and without; with a partial one they part after some tokens, as before: the CPU's experts do not round as the GPU's (open).
- **The tiles' transposition lighter: its weights asked for ahead, its loops unrolled, a block's 256 tokens at once**: the transposition of a chunk of eight rows waited for memory (62% of its time on its loads, perf, Q4_K on 24 vectors) while the tiles that followed left memory idle: the next chunk's bytes are now prefetched while a chunk's tiles compute (`gemm_ahead`, not Q8_0's: 3-4% slower there), the transposition's loops are unrolled so its registers no longer go through the stack, and the tiles hold the sums of 256 tokens, a prompt's block on the CPU, not 64 (32 for the types in blocks of 32), so each chunk is transposed once a block instead of four to eight times. On 32 vectors (12 threads) Q4_K 8.4 to 9.5 GB/s, Q3_K 5.6 to 6.4, IQ4_XS 7.8 to 9.0, Q5_K 10.5 to 11.4; on 256 6-15%. A prompt on the CPU with the experts in memory: Qwen3-30B-A3B UD-Q3_K_XL (1024 tokens) 116.4-117.7 to 121.4-123.4 tokens/s, Qwen3-4B Q4_K_M (512) 115.2 to 121.2-121.4. The same bits (`test_quant`'s rows to 300 vectors, past a block of 256; a mutation of the blocks' index caught). `bench_kernels` takes up to 512 vectors.
- **Q2_K and the IQ types with grids as tiles** (IQ3_S, IQ3_XXS, IQ2_XXS, IQ2_XS, IQ2_S, IQ1_S, IQ1_M): through Q6_K's tiles, a group of 16 weights a scale; the IQ types' weights as signed bytes (level times sign; with AVX-VNNI plus 128, the offset taken off with the scales' sums of activations, with AVX2 by sign and abs), their scales 1 + 2 s, the block's d with its factor; Q2_K's values 0 to 3, its mins' sums apart. The same bits (`test_quant`'s 11 rows to 65 vectors with all eight, AVX2 and AVX-VNNI; mutations of the offset, of abs and of Q2_K's dmin caught). From 12 vectors; on 64 (12 threads) 1.6 to 2.6 times as fast: IQ1_M 0.6 to 1.6 GB/s with AVX-VNNI, IQ3_XXS 1.2 to 2.8, Q2_K 1.5 to 2.6; with AVX2 1.5 to 2.2 times.
- **Q2_0 as tiles, and rows that end inside a group of 256 by rows**: Q2_0's two-bit values (the weight plus 1) go through the tiles of the types in blocks of 32, a block of 64 two of them with its d in both, from 24 vectors with AVX-VNNI (on 64, 12 threads, 1.7 to 2.1 GB/s); with AVX2 its products on reordered activations stay, the tiles never faster. Rows that end inside a group of 256 - Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's down experts, 640 columns - no longer go one by one on many vectors: the whole groups of all the rows, then the tails padded 16 rows at a time, both through the rows' products and their tiles (`janas_dot_multi_rows_n`); the down matrix (2560 rows of 640, one core) 1.5 times as fast on 8 vectors, 2.4 on 64 with AVX-VNNI, 1.7 with AVX2. The same bits (`test_row_tails` on 33 vectors and 37 rows; mutations of Q2_0's offset and of the tails' sum caught).
- **IQ4_XS as tiles too**: its signed values from the IQ4 table transposed, a signed scale a 32-weight group; with AVX-VNNI the values plus 128 by vpdpbusd, a multiply by the scales, 128 times the scales' sums of activations taken off; with AVX2 sign and abs, the scales by madd; one fma a block as before, the same bits. From 12 vectors; on 64 with AVX-VNNI 2.4 times as fast (12 threads), with AVX2 39%. Qwen3.8-27B UD-Q4_K_XL (IQ4_XS where its rows are many), prompt of 256 tokens with every tile type, AVX-VNNI: 9.0 to 15.6 tokens/s. The tiles' scales are made with vectors (an 8 x 8 transpose of the rows' scale pairs): Q3_K's tiles 19% faster on 24 vectors.
- **The types in blocks of 32 as tiles too** (Q8_0, IQ4_NL, Q4_0, MXFP4, Q5_0, Q4_1, Q5_1): each block's values unpacked to bytes with an offset (Q4_0's 8 and Q5_0's 16 as stored, MXFP4's 12, Q8_0's and IQ4_NL's 128 with AVX-VNNI) taken off with the block's activation sum; with AVX2 the small values' maddubs summed in int16 (eight for Q4_0 and Q4_1, four for the others), Q8_0 and IQ4_NL by sign and abs; the floats theirs, a sum per block position added as janas_hsum8, the same bits. On 64 vectors with AVX2 (12 threads) from 28% (Q8_0, IQ4_NL) to 70% (MXFP4) faster; with AVX-VNNI, whose rows' products had no reduction tree, from 24 vectors, 8% (Q8_0) to 33% (Q5_1). Gemma-4-26B-A4B UD-Q4_K_M's prompt of 512 tokens with every tile type, AVX2: 45.3 to 58.4 tokens/s.
- **Q3_K as tiles too**: its three-bit values (the weight plus 4) through Q6_K's tiles, four maddubs summed in int16 before a madd (AVX2), the -4 folded as Q6_K's -32; the same bits. Qwen3-30B-A3B UD-Q3_K_XL (its experts Q3_K), prompt of 512 tokens: 57.9 to 67.5 tokens/s with AVX2, 69.0 to 78.6 with AVX-VNNI.
- **Q5_K as tiles too**: five-bit values unpacked to bytes and transposed, four maddubs summed in int16 and one madd by the scales, twice a 32-weight piece (AVX2), or eight vpdpbusd and one multiply (AVX-VNNI); the mins and floats Q4_K's, the same bits. Qwen3.5-9B Q4_K_M (its attention and recurrent projections Q5_K), prompt of 512 tokens with every tile type: 30.7 to 43.3 tokens/s with AVX2, 38.3 to 57.5 with AVX-VNNI.
- **Q6_K and Q6_K_P as tiles too**: the eight rows' six-bit values unpacked to bytes (from Q6_K, or from Q6_K_P's planes at its level) and transposed the same way, a 16-weight group four dwords: two maddubs summed in int16 and one madd by the scales (AVX2), or four vpdpbusd and one multiply (AVX-VNNI); the same bits (`test_q6k_tiles`: 11 rows, 9 blocks, 1 to 65 vectors, every level, also under Wine). With Q4_K's tiles, Qwen3-4B Q4_K_M's prompt of 512 tokens: 58.7 to 79.1 tokens/s with AVX2 (1.33 times llama.cpp's on the same machine), 73.0 to 103 with AVX-VNNI; Qwen3.5-9B Q4_K_M, the model of issue #45, 30.7 to 40.1 with AVX2. `JANAS_Q4K_GEMM` is now `JANAS_CPU_TILES`.
- **A prompt's Q4_K products on the CPU as tiles of eight rows**: on eight or more tokens the Q4_K rows are taken eight at a time, transposed four bytes at a time into a small buffer once for 64 tokens, and multiplied by tiles of tokens with every lane a row - a 32-weight piece eight maddubs summed in int16 and one madd by the scales (AVX2), or eight vpdpbusd and one multiply (AVX-VNNI), and no horizontal sums; the same integer sums and float operations, so the results are the other kernels' to the bit (`test_quant` on 1 to 65 vectors and 11 rows, also under Wine). Qwen3-4B Q4_K_M's prompt of 512 tokens on the laptop's CPU, 12 threads: 57.1 to 72.0 tokens/s with AVX2 (llama.cpp on the same machine: 56.0, 59.4 on 6 threads), 71.2 to 92.2 with AVX-VNNI; Qwen3-30B-A3B Q4_K_M 54.6 to 60.7 (AVX2) and 63.9 to 70.2 (AVX-VNNI). `JANAS_CPU_TILES=0` keeps the rows' products. `JANAS_ROW_PIECE_KB` (off by default, not measured where it may count) takes a chunk's rows in pieces that stay in the level-2 cache, for CPUs with little of it (issue #45).
- **A mixture whose experts the card holds all: its pass in one submission, the plan on the GPU**: when the tier has a slot for every expert, every one is read into it at the first pass (layer by layer, the next one read ahead) and `moe_plan.comp` writes after each router what the CPU's plan wrote between two submissions - each row's token, each pair's row, the products' tables and tiles, the dispatches' sizes - the same words in the same places; the 49 submissions a token, each waited for, become one. Qwen3-30B-A3B UD-Q3_K_XL as a card of its own on the laptop's Arc, every expert in the tier: one token at a time 10.5 to 13.3 tokens/s, blocks of 16 50.2 to 53.9; the logits the same to the bit as with the CPU's plan, token by token and in blocks; drafts and none the same tokens; no error under the validation layer. The time between segments, measured past the tier's filling, was 16 ms of 88 a token, 2 of them the CPU's plan and the rest the submissions' round trip. `JANAS_GPU_GPLAN=0` keeps the CPU's plan; a watched router (`janas_llm_model_route_observer`) keeps it too.
- **MXFP4 and Q2_0 experts the same bits on the CPU and the card**: on a card of its own a mixture's MXFP4 and Q2_0 experts go through `b32_matvec.comp`'s rows reading the plan's table, as Q3_K, Q4_K and Q6_K's already did, instead of `b32_mmid.comp`'s tiles; Qwen3.6-35B-A3B MXFP4_MOE as a card of its own on the laptop's Arc: the logits the same to the bit with tiers of 1 and 2 GiB, drafts and none the same tokens, no error under the validation layer, one token at a time 4.1 to 4.5 tokens/s with a tier of 1 GiB. Q2_0's rows of 640 take the tail of `b32_matvec.comp`; Qwen3.8-Flash-Next is not yet taken whole by a card of its own, so that path is compiled, not run.
- **The CPU's plan in the token profile**: `JANAS_GPU_TOKEN_PROFILE=1` prints the time of a mixture's plans on the CPU beside the time between two segments; on Qwen3-30B-A3B with every expert in the tier, past the tier's filling, 2.1 ms of the 16 ms a token between segments: the rest is the submissions' round trip.
- **The GPU's timestamps of a mixture's layers**: `JANAS_GPU_TOKEN_PROFILE=1` now times a mixture's segments too, with the router, the experts' sum and the time between two segments (the CPU's plan, the submission's round trip) as phases of their own.
- **The state read back right from a cached stage**: the Y stage may be cached and not coherent (it is on the laptop's Arc): a read-back is invalidated, an upload flushed - without it the CPU read stale state and wrote only token 0.
- `llm_gen`: `LLM_GEN_DUMP=<path>` writes the first run's tokens.

## [2026-10-24] - The GPU checked when it opens, and gpu_check diagnoses by itself

- **gpu_check finds the faulty shader by itself**: every product runs twice, all its rows on the GPU and then shared with the CPU as the engine does, and nothing stops at the first error (it stopped after ten, which on an Arc B580 were all one Q6_K shape). Every wrong product is counted under the shader that computed it (`janas_gpu_last_shader`: name, vectors a workgroup, subgroup size), with its worst difference in units in the last place - a few: the driver rounds a float operation differently; many: wrong values - and the first wrong row with both values in hexadecimal (the printed ones looked equal on a Radeon 890M). Then it runs each wrong product again with the other shaders the engine could take for it (`janas_gpu_set_path`: the subgroup-per-row shaders and the ones with the lanes on the vectors off) and with every other subgroup size the device allows, and prints a summary to paste into an issue. The device line names the driver, its version and the float controls. `GPU_CHECK_TYPES=` checks some types only.
- **The engine checks the GPU when it opens it**: a product of every type the GPU takes, on the vector counts that pick each of its shaders, against the CPU's, bit for bit (small random matrices, 110 ms on the Arc of the development laptop, 270 ms as a card of its own). A shader this driver gets wrong is left out - the subgroup-per-row ones, the ones with the lanes on the vectors, q4k_mv, each family with another shader for the same products - and a type still wrong without them goes to the CPU; the status line says so (`GPU present (active, ...; the subgroup-per-row shaders (wrong on this driver))`). On the Arc B580 of issue #43 a Q6_K shader gave text with no sense; with the check the answers would be right from the first run. The result is kept in `~/.cache/janas/gpu_selfcheck` under the device, its driver's version, the subgroup size and Janas's version: once per driver. `JANAS_GPU_SELFCHECK=0` leaves the check out, `=2` runs it again. Tested with a Q6_K row shader broken on purpose at subgroups of 32, as a card of its own: found in 312 ms and left out, and the next opening read the decision from the cache.
- **`janas-diag` packages**: `vkcheck` and `gpu_check` alone, without the engine and with a page of their own, for Linux and Windows in every release (under 1 MB on Linux).
- **The sums of a row's tail kept apart from its products on any GPU**: in `q8_0_matvec.comp` and `b32_matvec.comp` the accumulators and their sums are `precise`, so that no compiler may fuse a sum with the product before it - the CPU rounds both - where a tail's accumulators restart from zero inside the loop (Q8_0 2560x640 one bit off on a Radeon 890M, issue #46, the likely cause). The same bits on the Arc of the development laptop, in place and as a card of its own, subgroups of 8 and 32.

## [2026-10-24] - Faster kernels on the CPU: Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's Q2_0 experts, IQ2 and IQ1, Q2_K, prompts of Q4_K and of the types in blocks of 32

- **A reply 13% faster, a prompt 7.5% faster, the same bits**: on the CPU (Core Ultra 9 185H, 12 threads, a 12 GiB expert cache), 1,024 tokens of Italian prose and then 200 generated, two runs each alternated with the release of 24 October, a reply went from 10.7-11.0 to 12.3-12.5 tokens/s and the prompt from 24.2 to 26.1; the experts of a reply from 30 ms a token to 19.8.
- **Q2_0 rows against activations reordered once for all the rows**: a Q2_0 byte holds four weights one apart, so each row was unpacked into the activations' order with shuffles, and the eight sums of 32 of each group of 256 reduced with six hadd. Now the activations are laid out as the weights' bit planes once per call (`q2_0_xperm`), each row's 16 bytes become two registers with two variable shifts and two ands, and two hadd are enough. One vector: 7.7 GB/s on a core and 34 on twelve threads before, 19 and 69 now; two, four and eight vectors (a prompt's experts, `janas_dot_multi_rows`): 1.8, 1.7 and 1.5 times as fast. Sums of integers in another order and the same float operations: equal to the scalar reference to the bit.
- **The IQ2 and IQ1 kernels 2.5 to 4.5 times as fast, Q6_K's on 2-8 vectors 8-12%**: the loops over a group's four sub-groups (IQ2_XXS/XS/S, IQ1_S/M) and over Q6_K's two halves were left rolled by GCC, their grid entries, signs and scales kept in memory; unrolled, one vector on a core went from 1.0 to 3.0 GB/s (IQ2_XXS), 1.2 to 5.4 (IQ2_S), 0.9 to 3.0 (IQ1_S), 0.6 to 2.3 (IQ1_M), and on twelve threads from 7.0 to 14.1, 8.4 to 20.9, 5.7 to 13.4 and 4.0 to 11.1. IQ1_M's scale is converted by F16C as the others', not by a call in the loop. The same bits.
- **Q2_K a third faster on one vector**: a block's sixteen scales are spread to its sub-groups by one shuffle each from a register, not loaded and broadcast one by one, and a vector's two sums reduced together. One vector from 8.6 to 12.9 GB/s on a core and from 37 to 49.5 on twelve threads, 2-8 vectors 8-20% (the UD-Q2_K_XL quantizations). The same bits.
- **Q4_K on 5-8 vectors 9-13% faster, Q5_K 6%** (`janas_dot_multi_rows`): what the products need of the vectors alone - their scales, their sums of 32 two by two laid out one int32 lane a vector - is made once for all the rows of a call, so that a row's mins on eight vectors are four madd where they were eight and six hadd, and its scales one load where they were eight. On eight vectors 5.7 to 6.45 GB/s on a core, 23.1 to 25.1 on twelve threads. A prompt of 512 tokens on the CPU against the release of 24 October, with the IQ and Q6_K changes above: Qwen3-4B Q4_K_M 61.4-62.5 to 73.2-73.4 tokens/s, Qwen3-30B-A3B 59.0-59.7 to 63.0-63.8; replies the same or 1-2% faster. The same bits.
- **The types in blocks of 32 about twice as fast on 2-8 vectors with AVX-VNNI** (Q8_0, Q4_0, IQ4_NL, MXFP4, Q5_0, Q4_1, Q5_1: a prompt's products): each group's eight blocks transposed in registers and the vectors' activations transposed once for all the rows of a call, so that eight `vpdpbusd` leave each block's sum in its own lane - no sign tricks and no tree of six hadd a vector. The signed weights go unsigned with an offset (128, or Q4_0's 8 and Q5_0's 16) and the offset times each block's sum of activations is taken off, exactly. On twelve threads and eight vectors Q8_0 from 25.5 to 52 GB/s, Q4_0 14.0 to 28.4, IQ4_NL 14.0 to 26.7, MXFP4 14.2 to 25.7, Q5_0 17.2 to 29.1, Q4_1 16.2 to 26.5, Q5_1 17.0 to 26.2; on two vectors Q4_0 40 to 68, Q8_0 78.5 to 87.6. Without AVX-VNNI, and on one vector, as before. The same bits.
- **IQ3_XXS 40-48% faster, IQ2_XXS and IQ2_XS 30-55%**: the signs of a 7-bit index, with the parity that completes them, read as eight bytes of multipliers from a table of 128 made at start (as ggml's ksigns), four at a time, instead of worked out and spread to bytes in the loop. One vector on a core IQ3_XXS from 4.65 to 6.9 GB/s, IQ2_XXS 3.0 to 4.8, IQ2_XS 3.45 to 4.8; on twelve threads 17.5 to 24.5, 14.2 to 19.6, 14.6 to 19.0. IQ2_S, whose signs are stored as bytes with no parity, keeps its way. The same bits.
- `tests/bench_kernels` measures every type the CPU multiplies (Q5_0, Q2_0, Q2_K, IQ3_XXS, IQ2_XXS/XS/S, IQ1_S/M added), with the IQ types' block scales normal fp16 numbers as in real weights (random bytes made some subnormal, and the float products slow), and the types named alone with `BENCH_TYPES=Q4_K,Q2_0`.
- `test_quant` checks the rows of Q2_0 against 1-17 vectors at once, and the rows of Q2_0, the K types and the types in blocks of 32 on 2, 48 and 130 groups of 256 (the vectors made ready a few at a time, and not at all), against the single products and the reference.

## [2026-10-24] - The chat's text on a Windows console in any language, gpu_check in the packages, the NPU made ready sooner

- **Fixed: janas-chat on a Windows console printed every character past ASCII as mojibake** under a locale whose code page is not UTF-8 (issue #44, Ukrainian: "в–„" for "▄"). The chat asked for the user's locale, which on Windows it never used - the widths of characters are counted there without it - and msvcrt then translated what was written to the console from that locale's code page. Not asked for on Windows any more; the rest of the systems as before.
- **`gpu_check` in the Linux and Windows packages**: every kind of product the GPU takes, run on the GPU Janas would use and compared with the CPU's, its last line `ok` or `FAILED` with the shapes that differ above it ([hardware](docs/hardware.md)); for the cards that answer wrong with the GPU and right with `JANAS_GPU=0` (issue #43).
- **The NPU made ready on the compute threads**: each matrix it takes dequantized and quantized again to INT8 a few rows a thread, the BF16 mixes turned to FP16 the same way, its inputs and outputs moved on the threads too - each value as before, so the graphs already compiled are found in the cache again. Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's 422 matrices went from 19.3 s to 11.4 s, the wait the chat shows as "preparing the NPU".

## [2026-10-23] - Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's mixes of the streams faster on the CPU, at eight bits faster than BF16

- **A reply 8.7% faster, a prompt 10% faster, the same bits**: on the CPU (Core Ultra 9 185H, 12 threads, a 12 GiB expert cache), 1,024 tokens of Italian prose and then 200 generated, two runs each alternated with the commit before, a reply went from 10.07 to 10.95 tokens/s and the prompt from 21.75 to 24.0; the mixes of the streams from about 30 ms a token to 19.7. Against llama.cpp, 400 tokens of C and 400 of Italian prose, every position gives the same digits as before.
- **Products of four rows or four tokens with their sums in registers**: GCC did not unroll the short loops over rows and tokens, kept the accumulators in memory and made every fused multiply-add a load and a store; unrolled, the Q8_0 mixes' product read from L3 at 40 GB/s ran at a quarter of its speed before. Among them `janas_bf16_dot4`, the four tokens of a prompt's BF16 products.
- **A single token's rows four at a time** (`janas_bf16_dot4r`, `janas_q8w_dot4r`), so that their chains of fused multiply-adds overlap, and rows shorter than 2 KB with their weights prefetched 2 KB ahead, where the next rows lie: a mix's up (10240 x 320) on 12 threads from 131 to 93 us in BF16, from 107 to 56 in Q8_0; its down (320 x 10240) at 82 GB/s.
- **Rows claimed as a share of those left** in the BF16 and Q8_0 products (4 to 256, as `matvec.c` claims them), not in fixed chunks of 16: a down of 320 rows no longer ends on threads waiting for the last chunks.
- **A mix's own work on the threads**: each stream of each token normalized by a thread of its own, a single token's mean split in pieces of 256, the rounding to bf16 eight at a time (`janas_round_bf16_n`): a single token's norm from 24.8 to 6.1 us, its mean from 8.6 to 5.2.
- **`JANAS_HC_MIX=q8` now faster than BF16**: a reply at 11.9 tokens/s (BF16 10.95; 7.68 before these changes), a prompt at 24.0; the scale of each Q8_0 block by F16C, exact. Its quality is as measured: the most likely token at 392 and 364 of 400 positions against BF16's 390 and 367. The default stays BF16.
- `tests/bench_long` and `tests/llm_eval` count the mixes in a phase of their own, `mix` (with the output's mix, which went into `output` before).
- `test_quant` checks the four-row products against one row at a time and the rounding to bf16 against the scalar rule, ties, infinities and NaNs among the values.

## [2026-10-23] - Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's prompt whole on the GPU, its mixes of the streams at eight bits

- **`JANAS_HC_MIX=q8` in the whole token on the GPU**: the mixes' down and up rewritten as Q8_0 are multiplied there too (`dense_mm.comp`'s third kind, Q8_0 weights on float inputs rounded to bf16), so the mixes at eight bits no longer keep a prompt's blocks on the CPU. Blocks of 256 tokens against llama.cpp: on C code the most likely token agrees at 247 of 256 positions (the BF16 mixes on the GPU 247), on Italian prose at 238 (230), and the mean divergence from llama.cpp's probabilities is the CPU's with the same mixes, KL 0.0180.
- **Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's sparse attention on the GPU too**: in each attention layer the indexer's keys and queries, the blocks the run completes, every token's scores of the blocks before it and its choice of the best 512 (a threshold found a byte at a time, the blocks in their order, those tied with it the earliest, as the CPU chooses) - `ix_prep`, `ix_pool`, `ix_score`, `ix_select` - and the attention over the positions chosen (`attn_decode.comp` and `attn_merge.comp` read them from a list; the dense positions are a list too). Past 2,051 positions a prompt's blocks no longer go back to the CPU. The blocks chosen are the CPU's, every one, at every token looked at. Against llama.cpp, the last 512 positions of 8,192 tokens - 512 of 2,048 blocks kept: the most likely token agrees at 481 (the CPU 482), mean logit difference 0.183 (0.191); of 2,200 tokens the 149 positions past the dense ones agree at all 149 (0.111; the CPU 0.117).
- **Fixed: after a prompt's blocks on the GPU the CPU's indexer keys were zero** (since the prompt went whole to the GPU, earlier today): every attention layer wrote its input for the indexer into the first layer's row, so past 2,051 positions the CPU chose its blocks from keys of zero, decoding too; at 8,192 tokens the most likely token agreed at 381 of 512 positions instead of 481.
- **Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's prompt blocks whole on the GPU** (the integrated GPU here, which shares the host's memory): its four streams, their mixes in BF16 (`hc_norm`, `hc_act`, `hc_mean`, `hc_comb`), the n-gram layer (`ng_gate`, `ng_conv`), the Q2_0 experts and the shared expert at their width of 640, a layer a submission with the CPU choosing the experts in between. A mixture whose cache holds fewer than half its experts, as here with 12 GiB, goes there with `JANAS_GPU_MOE=1`. Against llama.cpp, blocks of 256 tokens: on C code the most likely token agrees at 247 of 256 positions (the GPU's products beside the CPU, 248); on Italian prose at 230 (the same 235 and 238 in two runs, the CPU alone 235), and the mean divergence from llama.cpp's probabilities (KL) is the lowest of the three, 0.0172 against 0.0176 and 0.0186. Speed not measured yet.
- **Q8_0 rows ending inside a group of 256 on the GPU** (`q8_0_matvec.comp`): the whole groups' sum and then the tail's, as the CPU adds them, bit for bit (`gpu_check` now checks them); Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's shared experts' down matrices are such rows.
- **More than 256 experts at once from the cache** (`janas_expert_cache_fetch_many`): a block of 256 tokens of prose asked the first layer for 368 of its 512; requests of 256 that count as one, so that none takes a slot an earlier one gave. With `JANAS_GPU_VERBOSE` a block that goes back to the CPU says so.
- `tests/llm_eval` writes the logits of the blocks too (`bdump=F`).

- **`JANAS_HC_MIX=q8`**: the mixes of the four streams (each layer's two, and the output's), 1.26 GB of BF16 read at every token, rewritten as Q8_0 when the model opens - half the bytes - and multiplied by a product of their own, Q8_0 weights against float inputs rounded to bf16 as the BF16 products have them (`janas_q8w_dot`, eight AVX2 lanes, the same bits without AVX2; on the CPU). Against llama.cpp, 400 tokens of C and 400 of Italian prose: the most likely token agrees at 392 and 364 positions, mean logit difference 0.138 and 0.173, where in BF16 at 390 and 367 (0.135, 0.166). The way the other quantized products go - inputs quantized to eight bits - cost more than the weights' eight bits did: 388 and 359 (0.185, 0.204), whatever the weights' type (Q6_K gave the same), and four-bit weights (Q4_K, Q5_0) 382 and 349 even with float inputs; neither is kept. A prompt read in blocks gives the same bits as token by token, and the reply the same tokens with MTP drafts and without. The default stays BF16 until the speed is measured.
- **`--head 4` on an output head at five bits** (Q5_K, Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's): requantized to Q4_K at load as a Q6_K head is, 437 MB a token to 358; against llama.cpp 387 and 351 of 400 (0.184, 0.234), so a lever of the fast kind, off by default.

## [2026-10-22] - Qwen3.8-Flash-Next in janas-get, its long context checked, its memory counted

- **`janas-get qwen3.8-flash-next`**: downloads ISTA-DASLab's two-bit GGUF (two parts, 66.4 GB) and converts it here, then unsloth's GGUF of the MTP block from unsloth's repository, which `hf2jns_mtp` copies; every file checked against the SHA-256 in `MODELS.md`. Its weights are under the Qwen Community License 1.0, not Apache 2.0, and no converted file of it is published. The catalog takes an MTP block from a GGUF of its own in another repository (`JANAS_EXTRA_BLOCK`, `extra_repo`). Checked on the files already here: the model's fingerprint, the block's GGUF and the block converted, all as `MODELS.md` says.
- **`MODELS.md`**: Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's sources, revisions and fingerprints, and its licence in the paragraph on what this project redistributes; `docs/features.md`: what runs and what was checked.
- **The memory estimate** (`janas_llm_estimate`, and `janas_llm_open`'s automatic cache) counts qwen4exp's buffers for a block's residual streams and n-grams, about 94 MB on Qwen3.8-Flash-Next. Measured with a cache of 4 GiB, a context of 8,192 and a prompt of 4,873 tokens: 7.79 GiB estimated, 8.02 GiB at the peak; what is left over (0.2 GiB, there from the open: the tokenizer, converted copies of some weights) is not counted for any model, and the open leaves a fifth of the memory to other programs.
- **A long context checked**: 8,192 tokens, the last 512 positions against llama.cpp on the same file - the most likely token agrees at 482, mean logit difference 0.191; the same 512 tokens read after only 512 others: 482 again, 0.178. llama.cpp's own logits are the same read in batches of 256 and of 512. The prediction file is found above 2 GiB (`janas_llm_find_mtp`): a block of 3 GiB beside the model is taken, one larger than a quarter of the model is not.

## [2026-10-22] - Qwen3.8-Flash-Next in the chat: its reasoning, its tools, a prompt the same every run

- **The prompt Qwen3.8's template writes, token for token**: with the reasoning on, the template's words on the reasoning effort at the top of the system message ("Reasoning effort is set to xhigh. ...", read from the template, not written into Janas: Qwen3.8-27B's too), with or without a system message of the caller's, before the tools; and in a conversation loaded whole, every earlier reply of the assistant with its empty reasoning (the template's `preserve_thinking`), not only those after the user's last question as on Qwen3.5 and 3.6. Six cases checked against the template rendered by Jinja - reasoning on and off, a system message, a tool, a conversation with a call, its answer and a reply - the same tokens.
- **A service opened in the chat is then called, not opened again**: after `janas__open_service` the chat requires the next reply to call one of that service's tools. Qwen3.8-Flash-Next (two bits a weight) opened the `system` service again and again, until the chat stopped it - the service shares its name with its tools' prefix (`system__`), and the model's choice fell on "jan" at -0.27 against "system" at -1.44. `janas_llm_chat_tool_choice` takes a name with `JANAS_LLM_TOOLS_REQUIRED` for this: the calls only to the functions whose names begin with it (NULL or "" as before). The catalog of the services tells the model that most questions need none of them - writing, code, explanations - and the system service is "this computer itself, when the user asks about it": a question of C code opens nothing now, one about the free memory opens the service and calls its tool. An opened service's tools come one a line in `<tools>`, as the system message lists them.
- **`JANAS_GPU_REPEATABLE=1`**: every product of a mixture's experts on the CPU, exact, and in a prompt's blocks the GPU only with the fast prompt's own products, so the same request gives the same reply from one run to the next, and the same with drafts and without. By default an expert's product went to the GPU's rounded tiles when the GPU could read its slot of the cache, and to the CPU when it could not - which changes as the cache fills - and products shared with the CPU, begun as the tuner tried one configuration or another, left the GPU busy for the fast ones asked meanwhile: Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's reply to the same question changed from run to run, from the first token's probability on, with drafts or without them. Found by halves (the dense products, the tiles by weight type, rows ending inside a group), the CPU alone giving the same digits every run on the same busy machine. The three ways and their difference are in [features](docs/features.md). The chains of drafts the GPU verifies no longer take the experts' rounded products in one launch (a chain of 13 tokens or more on a mixture of ten experts a token did).
- Checked on Qwen3.8-Flash-Next: its reasoning (on by the chat's default for a reasoning model) closed by the model, kept apart from the answer, the same text with and without drafts on the CPU; the conversations kept in memory and on the disk (`tests/llm_keep`, which now takes `cache=MiB` for a machine busy with other work), the model's saved state with them.

## [2026-10-22] - Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's MTP block

- **Drafts from Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's own prediction block**, the one unsloth publishes beside its GGUF files (`MTP/mtp-Qwen3.8-Flash-Next-Q4_K_M.gguf`, 2.8 GB): `hf2jns_mtp` now takes such a file of the block alone, besides a checkpoint, and copies the block as it is, in its own types (Q4_K, Q5_0, Q6_K and Q8_0), into a file of one layer (1.9 GB, the same bytes on every run). The block works on the model's four streams, as llama.cpp's graph does: each stream of the last layer's residual normalized with weights of its own, beside the token's normalized embedding, and projected back a stream at a time - not pooled - into streams of its own; then its layer as the model's, its attention dense, its streams kept for the next draft of a chain, and a final mix of its own before the model's output head. On 158 positions of C code its guess was the model's next choice at 130 (82%), the text's next token at 116; a greedy reply of 48 tokens with chains of three drafts came out the same, token for token, as without them, 32 of its 51 drafts kept. In the chat, to `janas-bench`'s question on bread, 64 tokens of the reply with no reasoning: 29 of 40 drafts kept greedy (72.5%), 33 of 41 sampled as the chat samples (80.5%), each reply word for word the one written without drafts; unsloth measured 64.4% for its Q4_K_M block, greedy, with another file of the model and texts of its own. On the GPU with its products bit for bit (`JANAS_GPU_FAST_PROMPT=0`) the four replies are the CPU's own; with the fast prompt, the default, the GPU computes a group's experts in one dispatch, summed in another order, and the replies change, with drafts or without. With the NPU reading the model's blocks of the C code the guesses agreed at 130 of the 158 positions too. The block's own indexer for the sparse attention is copied but left unused, as llama.cpp leaves it.
- **An MTP file's experts are all kept in memory unless the caller asks for less** (`mtp_cache_bytes`): the memory the API plans for a model already counted the whole file. The old cache of 256 MiB held 74 of this block's experts of 3.6 MB, fewer than a block of tokens asks for at once (128), and the block failed; now a cache is never smaller than that. Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B's block keeps its 0.9 GB of experts whole too, where 256 MiB held 151 of its 512.
- **The chat finds a prediction file beside a large model up to a quarter of the model's size**, and at least 2 GiB as before: the fixed 2 GiB left out the block unsloth recommends for Qwen3.8-Flash-Next, its Q8_0 (2.6 GB). Qwen3.5-2B, Qwen3.5-9B, Gemma-4-E4B, Gemma-4-26B-A4B and Qwen3.8-Flash-Next find the files they found before, Qwen3-4B none; a file above 2 GiB was not tried, there being none here.
- `tests/mtp_eval` reads a row of hidden state as wide as the model gives it (`janas_llm_model_hidden_width`: Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's four streams, 10,240 numbers), takes the expert cache's size (`cache=MiB`), and says which step failed.

## [2026-10-22] - Q2_K and the IQ1, IQ2 and IQ3 types on the GPU

- **Weights in Q2_K, IQ2_XXS, IQ2_XS, IQ2_S, IQ3_XXS, IQ1_S and IQ1_M on the GPU**, the types of Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's and Qwen3.8-27B's other GSQ-RCO files and of unsloth's 1- and 2-bit ones: their products shared with the CPU, bit for bit as the CPU computes them (`iq2_matvec.comp`, `iq3xxs_matvec.comp`, `iq1_matvec.comp`, `q2k_matvec.comp`, with ggml's grids copied as data), a prompt's tiles (`iqk_mmq.comp`) and a mixture's experts in one dispatch (`iqk_mmid.comp`), which also takes experts in IQ3_S and IQ4_XS, until now left to the CPU. `tests/gpu_check` finds 980 more shapes the CPU's bits, `tests/gpu_fast_check` 60 more products within 1e-4 of the CPU's. No file of the catalog has these types: none of its models changes.

## [2026-10-22] - Qwen3.8-Flash-Next

- **Qwen3.8-Flash-Next (`qwen4exp`), a mixture of 512 experts of 177 billion parameters**, from ISTA-DASLab's GSQ-RCO files: its residual in four streams, mixed into the input of each layer's token mixer and feed-forward by gates of low rank in place of the norms, each sublayer's output added back to every stream with a weight of its own (hyper-connections); its table of n-gram embeddings at the second layer, 51 billion numbers read a row at a time from the file, sixteen rows of 160 a token; its DeltaNet layers' output gated by a sigmoid; and its attention's sparse selection, which past 2,051 positions attends only to the 512 blocks of four positions its indexer scores highest and to the query's own incomplete block. Against llama.cpp (build `2b18470`, the same Q2_0 file), the most likely token agreed at 390 of 400 positions of C code (mean absolute difference of the logits 0.135), at 367 of 400 of Italian prose (0.166), and at 2,171 of 2,200 positions of a longer text of code - all 148 past 2,051, where the attention is sparse (0.117). A pass over a block gives the same logits, bit for bit, as token by token, also when drafts are taken back.
- **Weights in Q2_0 (ggml's type 42: two bits in blocks of 64) and Q5_0**, and **IQ1_S, IQ1_M, IQ2_XXS, IQ2_XS, IQ2_S, IQ3_XXS and Q2_K**: the types of ISTA-DASLab's four GSQ-RCO files of Qwen3.8-Flash-Next and of Qwen3.8-27B, and of unsloth's 1- and 2-bit files. Unpacked to the same numbers as ggml's, bit for bit; their products on AVX2 and AVX-VNNI, on one to eight vectors, the same bits as the scalar reference.
- **Rows that end inside a group of 256 weights**: Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's experts are 640 wide, which only the types in blocks of 32 or 64 can hold. `gguf2jns` keeps that width (widened with zeros to 768, a fifth more bytes would be read at every token), and the products take the last group as one padded with zero blocks.
- A saved state of `qwen4exp` (the chat's conversations kept) holds what its n-gram embeddings and its indexer read back, and when the context fills up and the oldest exchanges are forgotten, the history of those embeddings and the indexer's keys move along with the rest. The memory the engine sets aside for a context counts those keys too, 4.5 KB a position, out of the expert cache's share.
- **Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's prompts on the GPU and on the NPU.** Blocks of 256 tokens against llama.cpp, the most likely token: on the GPU at 248 of 256 positions of C code and 232 of 256 of Italian, on the NPU at 252 and 236, where the CPU token by token gives 246 and 235. The GPU takes Q2_0 and Q5_0 in its products shared with the CPU, bit for bit as the CPU computes them, rows of 640 among them; in the fast prompt's tiles, and the experts of a block in one dispatch (`b32_mmid.comp`: Q2_0, Q5_0, IQ4_NL); and the bf16 mixes of the streams, 1.25 GB of weights read at every token. The NPU takes every type the engine unpacks, Q2_0, Q5_0 and the 1- to 3-bit kinds among them, rows of 640, a shared expert 640 wide in one run, and the bf16 mixes with their weights in FP16. `tests/gpu_check` covers the new types and rows, `tests/gpu_fast_check` the fast prompt's tiles of the blocks of 32 and 64 against the CPU. Not yet for it: the MTP block.
- **A reply's tokens on a card with memory of its own take the bf16 mixes too** (`bf16_matvec.comp`), bit for bit as the CPU computes them: its eight lanes of fused multiply-adds (`src/llm/bf16.c`), summed across a subgroup in the same order, the inputs and outputs through the GPU's stages. With the GPU taking single tokens (`--reply-on gpu`), Qwen3.8-Flash-Next gives the same logits over 32 tokens, byte for byte, as on the CPU alone - 219 bf16 products a token on the GPU - also with its weights copied into the GPU's memory as on a card (`JANAS_GPU_COPY=1`). `tests/gpu_check` compares 96 of them bit for bit, in place and through the stages. An integrated GPU, which does not take single tokens by default, is left as it was.
- **The NPU also takes the products whose outputs lie further apart than their rows** - a recurrent layer's q|k|v and z side by side in one buffer, on Qwen3.5, Qwen3.6 and Qwen3.8 - which stayed on the CPU: on Qwen3.6-35B-A3B its blocks of 256 give the CPU's most likely token at 251 of 256 positions. The memory set aside for the NPU's copies counts every type it takes (only Q4_K, Q5_K, Q6_K and Q8_0 were counted), so that a model of other types does not have them on top of its expert cache.
- **A per-layer table in memory where the machine holds it** - Gemma 4's per-layer embeddings, Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's n-grams (28.8 GB) - read into memory at load when what is free holds it beside a cache of every expert, read a row at a time from the file only where memory is short; `JANAS_PLE_RAM=1` asks for it, `0` never. Gemma 4 E2B gives the same logits, byte for byte, either way. The GPU is never given such a table, which it does not read: an integrated GPU's driver pins the pages of what it is given, the table's 28.8 GB among them.

## [2026-10-22] - Janas's classes for FreeBASIC

- **`include/janas/janas_oop.bi`: Janas-LLM and Janas-MCP with objects, for FreeBASIC** - `Janas.Model`, `Janas.Chat`, their options, a reply's counters, a conversation kept by the program, a handler that receives a reply as it is written through its overridden methods, and an MCP server's tools; objects that close what they hold at the end of their scope and cannot be copied, a Chat that knows when its Model closed; methods that return False and say why. `include/janas/janas.bi` takes `llm.bi` and `mcp.bi` in one include. `tests/test_fb`, in the suite where FreeBASIC is found, tries every class without a model; `tests/fb_check`, on a model, finds the classes' replies the library's own in C, byte for byte.
- **The chat in FreeBASIC moved from `tools/` to `examples/fb-chat/`**, beside its version with classes, `chat_oop.bas`; its counters' line no longer overflows past 99.9 tokens a second.

## [2026-10-22] - The module for Node.js in the Linux package

- **The Linux package carries `janas.node`** and its types for TypeScript, `janas.d.ts`, in `node/`: a Node.js program opens a model with the package alone, without building Janas. The release workflow checks that the module, as packed, loads, where its runner has Node.js. The Windows package has no module yet.

## [2026-10-21] - Janas-LLM in Node.js

- **`janas.node`, the library as a module for Node.js** (Linux), made by `./build.sh` beside the programs, with its types for TypeScript (`janas.d.ts`): `open()` a model, `model.chat()`, then `chat.send(text)` or `chat.load(messages)` and the reply read with `for await`, piece by piece (the answer, the reasoning, how much of a long prompt is read), or whole with `reply.text()`, its counters with `reply.stats()`. Leaving the loop, `reply.stop()` or an `AbortSignal` stops it. Each model runs on a thread of its own, so JavaScript's thread never waits for it; a model's replies come one at a time, in the order asked; an idle model does not keep Node running, and one left open is closed when Node leaves. Written in C against Node-API (version 8), whose functions it declares itself: building it needs nothing of Node's (checked against Node's headers where they are installed). Tried with Node 20: `tests/test_node` without a model, in the suite, and `tests/node_check` on Qwen3-0.6B, where the replies through Node are the library's own in C, byte for byte; both also under AddressSanitizer and ThreadSanitizer. `docs/library.md`, "From Node.js".
- **`examples/node-chat`, a chat in TypeScript on the module**: a server on `node:http` that opens the model in its own process and streams each reply to the browser as it is written (server-sent events), a page that keeps the conversation and shows the reasoning apart and what each reply cost, and the types of what goes between the two shared by both sides. A client that goes away stops its reply; requests from several pages wait in line, told how many are before them.

## [2026-10-21] - Intel's NPU reads the prompts

- **The NPU of Intel's Core Ultra processors reads a prompt's blocks** (Linux; tried on the first generation's, Meteor Lake, "Intel AI Boost"). Janas-LLM writes the NPU's graphs itself, in OpenVINO's IR format, and opens Intel's Level Zero driver when a model opens: nothing is linked against it, and without it nothing changes. The weights it takes are copied in eight bits with a scale for each row and computed in sixteen-bit floats, the norms in 32. A dense model whose layers fit in the NPU's memory, nine in ten at least, goes to it layer by layer, whole - Qwen3, Qwen3.5 and Gemma 4's dense models - each block of the prompt in two halves, the NPU computing a layer on one while the CPU computes the attention, or Qwen3.5's recurrence, on the other; a larger model, or a mixture of experts, gives it the products of the weights that stay in memory. On the development laptop, `janas-bench --prompt 1024 --gen 16 --rounds 2`, the best configuration with the NPU against the best without it (the GPU's fast prompt): Qwen3-0.6B 1,421 tok/s against 974, Qwen3.5-0.8B 1,403 against 950, Qwen3.5-2B 729 against 477, Gemma-4-E2B 508 against 196, Qwen3-4B 333 against 201, Gemma-4-E4B 245 against 129. A reply is written by the CPU as before.
- **On the same NPU, llama.cpp's OpenVINO backend** (build `2b18470`, OpenVINO 2026.4, `GGML_OPENVINO_DEVICE=NPU`): a prompt of 512 tokens of Qwen3-4B read at 120 tok/s, where Janas-LLM read it at 364, and Qwen3-0.6B at 419 against 1,542; it wrote at 5.0 and 12.0 tok/s, all on the NPU, where Janas-LLM wrote on the CPU at 26.6 and 136. The Qwen3.5 models and Gemma 4's did not run on it ("failed to decode prompt batch"), and from 768 tokens of prompt on it stopped on a segmentation fault.
- **The engine takes the NPU by itself only where it reads faster and its answers were measured as close to llama.cpp's as the CPU's**: against llama.cpp's scores on two texts, Italian prose and code, the most likely token agreed at as many positions with the NPU as with the CPU, give or take 7 of 512, on eight models (Qwen3.5-0.8B and 2B, Qwen3-4B, Gemma-4-E2B and E4B, Qwen3-30B-A3B, Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B). On Gemma 4's larger models, product by product, it agreed less (Gemma-4-26B-A4B 363 of 512 against 398): there it reads only when asked. On the mixtures and the dense models too large for it the GPU's fast prompt read faster on this laptop (Qwen3-30B-A3B 133 tok/s against 90-110 with the NPU), and the engine kept the GPU.
- **The NPU is left off where its copies would take the expert cache's memory**: on the mixtures whose experts do not all fit in memory (Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B, Qwen3-Coder-Next, Qwen3.6-35B-A3B) and on Qwen3.8-27B, its eight-bit copies came out of the cache, and Qwen3.8-27B wrote at 1.7 tok/s instead of 4.4. There it opens only when `--prompt-on` names it.
- **The chat shows the NPU being made ready.** The first blocks it reads in a session wait while its weights are copied and its graphs compiled - 7.5 s for Qwen3-0.6B, 17 for Qwen3.5-2B, 24 for Gemma-4-E2B, about 50 for Qwen3-4B and Gemma-4-E4B - and the status line says how far it is ("preparing the NPU 12/25, 8 s"), wherever it happens: while the system message is read ahead, or in a reply; after the reply a line says how long it took. `janas_llm_npu_preparing` (new in `llm.h` and `llm.bi`) gives the same to any program, from another thread while the engine works. A block read while the expert cache still fills goes without the NPU, rather than make graphs that the whole layers drop a moment later (5 s of Qwen3.5-2B's first block).
- **What runs where, by hand**: `--prompt-on`, `--drafts-on` and `--reply-on` in `janas-chat`, `janas-server` and `janas-bench` (and `JANAS_PROMPT_ON`, `JANAS_DRAFTS_ON`, `JANAS_REPLY_ON`) take `auto` (the default), `cpu`, `gpu`, `npu`, two joined by `+` to work in the same pass (`npu+gpu`), or a list apart by commas the engine measures and chooses from (`npu,gpu`); the CPU takes part in every pass, the NPU in prompts only. `/save-config` keeps them. `--npu-int8` reads with eight-bit activations on the dense Qwen3 models, an experiment: Qwen3-4B 387 tok/s against 333, its scores further from the CPU's (a mean difference of 0.60 against 0.33, measured on 20 October).
- **`janas-bench` measures the NPU's configurations**, "+ NPU" and "+ GPU + NPU", prepares it in a warm-up of its own, outside the measures, and says when the engine would not choose it by itself on that model. Its line `NPU:` names the NPU, or says why there is none to use.
- **A mixture's Q3_K experts go to the GPU** in the fast prompt (`q3k_mmid.comp`, one launch for a layer's experts, as Q4_K's, Q6_K's and MXFP4's), and with a whole token on the GPU (`--reply-on gpu`). Unsloth's 3-bit Qwen3-30B-A3B read a prompt of 1,024 tokens at 119.5 tok/s, where it read 98.5 with its experts on the CPU and llama.cpp reads 114.3: the one model of the README's table on which llama.cpp read faster no longer is. Given the CPU's quantized activations the new shader agrees with the CPU's products to their rounding: against llama.cpp's scores of Qwen's Q8_0 the most likely token agreed at 220 and 249 of 256 positions (Italian prose, code), against the exact way's 221 and 245.
- **An expert cache reaches the GPU in regions below 4 GiB**: Mesa's Intel driver passes the size of an imported allocation on to the kernel in 32 bits, and a region of 4 GiB exactly - the second of unsloth's 3-bit Qwen3-30B-A3B, whose slots of 2,236,416 bytes fit 1,920 times 1 MB short of it - went as 0 bytes and was refused: the experts stayed on the CPU, with nothing said. None of the other mixtures measured had such a region. `JANAS_GPU_VERBOSE=1` now says why an import is refused, and where.
- **The fast prompt's distance from the exact way, measured again**, on Qwen3-30B-A3B against llama.cpp's scores of Qwen's Q8_0, Italian prose and code: the most likely token within 5 of 256 positions either way (unsloth's 3-bit file 216 and 246 against the exact way's 221 and 245, Qwen's Q4_K_M 210 and 239 against 207 and 244), the mean difference of the scores within 3%. `docs/features.md` said within 2-3 positions and 2%, from the models and texts measured on 10 October; on these two texts 8 to 27 of the 256 positions change their most likely token with the rounding, and they fell as far as 5 either side.
- **The drafts in the chat as in `janas-bench`.** The planner that chooses how many of the MTP block's drafts to verify learned what a pass costs from every pass, and in a new conversation the tuner tries each configuration in turn on the passes of 2 to 16 tokens, those that verify drafts: from the slow ones it tried (279-357 ms a pass where its choice takes 50) the planner learned that the drafts did not pay, and drafted one pass in sixteen. It now learns only from the passes run on the tuner's choice, without what they waited for the disk, and learns the costs again at each turn. Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B in the chat, a reply of 64 tokens with the cache warm: 31.2 tok/s with 32 drafts, where it came at 24.7 with 4; the first reply of a session 19.3 against 16.0; over five seeds 30.9-35.3 tok/s, the median 31.4.
- **The thread that keeps the NPU awake sleeps while it is not needed**: it woke every half millisecond for as long as the program ran, 2,000 times a second beside the cores writing a reply.
- **`janas_aligned_alloc` asks for a size the alignment divides**, as C11 wants: ThreadSanitizer refused `test_aligned`'s one byte on eight.
- **A research probe, merged though not final**, off unless its variables are set: Ana-Net (the experts' down matrices by neuron, `JANAS_Q6_K_PN` from `tools/jns_neurons.c`, each token's neurons read at 6, 4 or 2 bits or not at all, `JANAS_NEURON_LEVELS`; records for the predictor's tests, `JANAS_ANA_*`).
- **The README**: the banner back at the top; one table of the speeds, each engine at its best and the configuration that gave Janas-LLM its figure; the NPU, how it works, and llama.cpp's OpenVINO backend on it; the options that choose what runs where; the measurement notes of 21 October, and the conditions of the drafts' figures of 10 October beside the chat's of 21 October.

## [2026-10-18] - Qwen3.6-35B-A3B's expert cache holds what it has room for

- **An MTP block inside the model's file no longer halves the expert cache.** Qwen3.6-35B-A3B's files carry the block that drafts tokens as a last layer whose experts are in Q8_0, 3,342,336 bytes each against about 2 MB for the model's own, and every slot of the cache was as large as the largest expert: the cache held little more than half the experts its memory had room for. The block's experts now have slots of their own at the end of the same memory, out of the same budget (room for one request, 128 experts, or for all of them when the budget holds every expert), and the model's experts slots of their size; the automatic cache, `janas_llm_estimate` and `janas_llm_memory` count the new layout, and nothing changes where the block's experts are no larger. Qwen3.6-35B-A3B Q4_K_M with a 14 GiB cache, `janas-bench --prompt 512 --gen 128 --ctx 4096`, two series of two runs each alternated with the version before: prompt 132-133 tokens/s against 75-81, generation 27.3 against 18.1-18.9, with MTP drafts 39-43 against 32-33, the cache serving 98.6% of the experts against 91-92%. The answers are the same bit for bit (janas-try's fingerprints unchanged). Found by Rendeverance (#34); the fix is written anew.
- **Windows: no fault freeing a block that starts on a page.** `free` looked for an alignment tag before every block it was given, and read the page before one that began on a page boundary, which may not be mapped: an access violation, seen on Windows on ARM64 (found by Rendeverance, #31). Blocks from the aligned allocator now go back through `janas_aligned_free`, the only one that reads the tag; the asan build uses the tagged blocks on Linux too, so a block given back the wrong way stops the tests (`tests/test_aligned`).
- **build.sh stops when a source listing fails**, naming the directory or the file, instead of linking a library short with no message; a library of others with no flags of its own stops it too (found by phaedrus1992, #30).
- **The GPU code builds on ARM64 with the Vulkan headers installed**: it included the x86 intrinsics' header for one spin-wait hint.
- **Code from contributors waits** for the non-profit foundation that will hold Janas's copyright (CONTRIBUTING.md).


## [2026-10-17] - The same answer whatever the C library and the compiler

- **The engine computes exp, log, pow, sin, cos and tanh itself** (`src/llm/jmath.c`), in double with plain IEEE-754 operations, where it called the C library's: the rotary angles, the softmax of the experts' router and of the attention's pieces, the DeltaNet's softplus, decay and gates, the soft cap of the logits, the sampler. The C library's functions differ between versions and systems - glibc 2.41 rounds several of them correctly where 2.39 did not - and one unit in the last place was enough to turn a near tie between two tokens the other way: Qwen3.6-35B-A3B's answer at temperature 0 was another text on Ubuntu 24.04 (issue #38, reported by martinambrus). The float results are the double ones rounded once; `tests/test_jmath` checks them against the C library in double over the ranges the engine uses them on (exp within 0 units in the last place, the others within one).
- **No compiler fuses a multiply and an add on its own** (`-ffp-contract=off`): gcc did in the kernels' functions compiled for FMA, each version where it saw fit - eight places, all in the scalar ends of vector loops; the fused operations the kernels write themselves stay.
- **janas-try's answer fingerprints**: medium's is `13e2f198c270b826` and gemma's `bf1626afffa7eeab` now, quick's and full's unchanged, each the same with the GPU, without it and with the AVX2 kernels forced. Against llama.cpp, on eleven texts of six models (Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3.5-0.8B, 2B and 9B, Qwen3-4B against its own file and against the Q8_0's), the most likely token agrees at 4,111 of 4,400 positions, 4,108 before.
- **test_quant says whether it compared the scalar SiLU with the AVX2 one**: on a CPU without AVX2 and FMA it passed as if a million values had agreed, comparing none (issue #26, reported by phaedrus1992); it prints how many it compared, or that there was nothing to compare with.


## [2026-10-14] - A model's memory, before it opens and while it is open

- **A model's memory, before it opens and while it is open**: `janas_llm_estimate` gives what a model takes whatever its expert cache and what a cache holding all its experts takes, the reckoning `janas_llm_open` makes for its automatic cache; `janas_llm_memory` what an open model holds (resident weights, expert cache, keys and values).


## [2026-10-14] - A mixture's experts on the GPU beside a shared expert of another type

- **A layer's experts go to the GPU even where its shared expert is of another type.** Where the expert cache holds less than half a mixture's experts, a prompt's experts go to the GPU a layer's group at a time, and a group opens with the layer's shared expert; when its type differed from the routed experts' - Q8_0 beside Qwen3.6-35B-A3B's Q4_K or MXFP4 experts, a plain Q6_K down beside Qwen3-Next's - the whole group stayed on the CPU. Now the CPU computes the shared expert beside the GPU's share. Qwen3.6-35B-A3B Q4_K_M with a 14 GiB cache: the prompt's rows with the GPU 3.7% faster (69.9 tokens/s against 67.3, four runs alternated), the most likely token agreeing with llama.cpp at 197 of 200 positions against 196.


## [2026-10-14] - MXFP4 on the GPU, and the experts of a large cache back on it

- **A mixture's experts reach the GPU again when the expert cache holds more than 4 GiB of them.** Since the release of 13 October the memory the GPU reads is given to it in regions that start on 2 MB boundaries, each overlapping the one before, and the overlap was not counted in the 4 GiB a region may take: past the first, a region could come to a little more, which the GPU refused, and the expert cache was then not given to it at all. On an integrated GPU a mixture's experts stayed on the CPU, in the fast prompt and in the block read whole on the GPU, whenever the cache held more than about 4 GiB of experts, depending on the size of an expert: Qwen3.6-35B-A3B's MXFP4_MOE file beyond 4 GiB, its Q4_K_M at six bits beyond 8 GiB. Each region counts its overlap now, and `tests/test_import_regions` checks them.
- **MXFP4 on the GPU**, which only the CPU read since this morning's MXFP4 products: the products of one vector to a few, to the same bits as the CPU; the fast prompt's tiles; and a mixture's MXFP4 experts in one launch per layer, the layer read whole on the GPU. noctrex's Qwen3.6-35B-A3B-MXFP4_MOE read in blocks of 100 with the GPU, against llama.cpp on the same file: 200 of 200 most likely tokens agree, mean logit difference 0.106 (on the CPU, token by token: 199, 0.105).


## [2026-10-14] - An integrated GPU no longer kept awake for single tokens

- **The GPU is kept awake only for the passes it takes.** On an integrated GPU a reply's single tokens stay on the CPU, but a configuration with the GPU kept it awake for them too - an empty dispatch every half millisecond - and on a laptop the awake GPU takes the power the cores need: janas-bench's "+ GPU" rows wrote a reply slower than the CPU alone, 10.5 tokens/s against 17.3 on a Radeon 780M (issue #16, reported by dlahoza), 2-5% slower on the development laptop's Arc. Now they write as fast (Qwen3-4B, 20, 12 and 6 threads: 28.5/28.1, 28.0/28.0 and 26.3/26.3 tokens/s with "+ GPU", 28.5/28.3, 28.1/28.0 and 26.3/26.3 without, two alternated runs). A chat's own choices were not affected: the tuner gives single tokens to the GPU only where it takes them, a discrete GPU. janas-bench's note says that the single tokens are the CPU's in every row where so.


## [2026-10-14] - MXFP4 experts, and output heads in BF16

- **The "MXFP4_MOE" files run.** Their routed experts are MXFP4 (OCP's microscaling format: 32 four-bit values and a power of two a block), which gguf2jns refused and the engine could not read. The engine has MXFP4 products now - IQ4_NL's kernel with the block's power of two for a scale, the same bits on AVX2 and AVX-VNNI, for one vector or eight, and the same bits as the exact Q8_0 rewrite of the same blocks - and gguf2jns keeps such experts as they are. noctrex's Qwen3.6-35B-A3B-MXFP4_MOE against llama.cpp on the same file: 199 of 200 most likely tokens agree, mean logit difference 0.105 (on Italian prose 63 of 64, 0.085), closer than bartowski's Q4_K_M to its own llama.cpp (196, 0.146; 60, 0.135). The file itself, against llama.cpp's Q8_0 of the same model, is further than that Q4_K_M (378 against 387 of 400 most likely tokens, 364 against 370 on Italian prose) and 9% smaller (20.3 GB against 22.3). It is also slower on the development laptop (janas-bench, two runs each in alternated order): a prompt at 85 tokens/s against 96, a reply at 25.3 against 27.0, and without drafts, where the Q4_K_M has its MTP block (38 tokens/s): its weights outside the experts are Q8_0 and the head BF16, 3.1 GB in the file where the Q4_K_M's are 1.9. On 12 threads of the development laptop the MXFP4 product reads 66 GB/s where IQ4_NL reads 73: 4% fewer weights a second, a block of it being 17 bytes to IQ4_NL's 18 (its scales come from a table: computed in the kernel, they took registers the unpacking needs and it ran 14% slower).
- **An output head in BF16 or F16 is read as Q8_0.** The "MXFP4_MOE" files keep theirs in BF16 (1,017 MB on Qwen3.6-35B-A3B), which the engine refused. It is rewritten once, as the model opens, the way llama.cpp's quantization makes Q8_0, into 540 MB - the head is read whole at every token - and the model's description says so; the file keeps the BF16. A token embedding table in BF16 or F16 is rewritten the same way.


## [2026-10-14] - janas-try's answers, and the GPU's subgroup operations

- **janas-try's answers match again, on every machine** (issue #23, reported by clethbridgedaniel): since 1 October janas-chat starts Janas's own services and writes their catalog into the system message, even with `--system ""`, and janas-try asked its fixed question that way, while the expected fingerprints date from 25 September: no level could match, on any machine. The engine was right to the bit. Built from clethbridgedaniel's commit, here on an Intel Core Ultra 9 185H with AVX-VNNI and GCC 14, CPU only, Qwen3-Next-80B answers with `f393d4bfb674402f`, the fingerprint the report gave from a Ryzen 9 5900HX with AVX2 and GCC 16 - the same without the MTP drafts and with the AVX2 kernels forced - and with `4aae064b918a0e75`, the expected one, without the services; Qwen3-4B likewise gives its expected `c08f0f936eeba5cb` without them. janas-try now asks without MCP servers and without the machine's measure (`--no-mcp --no-tune`), in configuration, cache and data directories of its own, so that neither settings saved with `/save-config` nor a system message kept from an earlier chat can change the answer. The medium level expects `e49b0016cedf6c50` now: Qwen3.6-35B-A3B's Q8_0 tensors take the activations with a scale per 32 since 27 September (the fix that brought Gemma-4-26B from 147 to 156 of 200 against an exact float64 pass), and janas-try still expected the answer from before. All four levels give their fingerprints here, with the GPU and without it.
- **A GPU without the subgroup operations the shaders use is turned down, and the reason names them** (`no clustered, shuffle subgroup operations in compute shaders`): besides ballot, 13 shaders use subgroup arithmetic, clustered reductions or shuffles, and the engine checked only ballot, so a driver without them would have been handed shaders it cannot build. `vkcheck` gives the same decision, where it used to warn that the engine did not check them yet.


## [2026-10-13] - A ceiling on the reasoning

- **The reasoning can be given a ceiling**: `max_reasoning` in the library's chat parameters, `--reasoning-budget` and `reasoning_budget_tokens` (or `thinking_budget_tokens`, the names llama.cpp takes) on the server, `--think N` and `/think N` in the chat. A model still reasoning there has its reasoning closed for it, a sentence ("I have thought enough; now I give the answer.") and the closing mark written as if it had written them, and goes on with its answer; with no ceiling nothing changes, the replies identical to the bit. The sentence is what makes a small model answer: cut at 32 tokens with janas-chat's system message, Qwen3.5-0.8B, Qwen3.5-2B and Qwen3-4B ended 20 of 36 replies themselves given the mark alone, the small ones going on reasoning in their answer and mostly looping to its end, and 35 of 36 given the sentence, the replies a third as long (96 tokens on average against 265); Qwen3-4B answered either way.


## [2026-10-13] - A build without the GPU, and a request that never ended

- **A build without GPU support links again** (issue #22, reported by mashirotamigi): two of the GPU's functions had no stand-in in such a build, and without `glslangValidator` the link failed.
- **A glslang too old for the shaders builds without the GPU, and says so**, where the build stopped at the first shader: the shaders need `GL_EXT_integer_dot_product`, which glslang knows from 15.2 on, and Ubuntu 24.04's is 15.1. `build.sh` tries `glslangValidator` on a probe first; the reason reaches the engine, which gives it when asked for the GPU. `INSTALL.md` says where to find a newer glslang on Ubuntu 24.04.
- **Issue #21, a server request that never ended on an RTX 5060 Ti with the tuner on, was issue #17's**: on a discrete card the tuner tries the GPU for single tokens too, whose results never reached the CPU, and the model's reasoning, garbled, never closed; in a chat `max_tokens` caps the answer, not the reasoning, so the reply ran towards the end of the context. Reproduced here on the path a discrete card takes: with the code before the fix the request had not ended after 180 s, the server at 57 minutes of CPU; with it, the right answer in 7.5 s.


## [2026-10-13] - vkcheck: can this GPU run Janas-LLM?

- **`vkcheck` tells whether a GPU can run Janas-LLM's GPU backend, and why not**: every requirement with the driver's answer (Vulkan 1.3, buffer addresses, the integer dot product, the subgroup operations the shaders use, the size of a subgroup, host memory imported in place), the decision the engine would take, then three tests on the device: zero-copy, the int8 dot product (exact, and a rough rate that shows whether the driver emulates it) and the float operations that keep the GPU's results the CPU's to the bit. On the development laptop's Arc everything passes, the dot product at 1,711 billion multiply-adds a second. It is a single C file that builds where Janas does not build yet, macOS with MoltenVK included: the first step of the arm64 and Apple Silicon port proposed in issue #20.


## [2026-10-13] - Qwen3-Coder-Next as llama.cpp writes it now

- **qwen3next models converted by llama.cpp since early 2026 load** (issue #18, reported by nica-f with unsloth's Qwen3-Coder-Next-Q3_K_M): the recurrent layers' input projection comes as two tensors, `attn_qkv` and `attn_gate`, where the engine wanted the single `ssm_in`, its rows interleaved per key head. Both layouts are read; Qwen3-Coder-Next rewritten in the new one gives the very logits of the old, to the bit, token by token and in blocks.
- **MXFP4 tensors written as Q8_0, exactly**: the same file has its shared experts in MXFP4 (ggml's type 39), which the engine did not know. An MXFP4 weight is an integer of -12 to 12 times its block's power of two, and Q8_0 holds both, so `gguf2jns` rewrites such a tensor with every weight unchanged, at twice the bytes (80 MB more for Qwen3-Coder-Next's shared experts). Checked on a Qwen3-4B with four layers' attention in MXFP4: the converted tensors give the GGUF's weights to the bit. Routed experts in MXFP4 are refused with a message for now: twice their bytes on every token would undo what the format is for.


## [2026-10-13] - janas-bench says what the GPU does

- **`janas-bench` says that the GPU reads a prompt in every configuration** (issue #16, a Radeon 780M): with the fast prompt a prompt's blocks of 64 tokens or more go to the GPU whether a row says "+ GPU" or not, so that a prompt gives the same results in each, and the rows without it had been read as the CPU alone, the GPU as of no help where it read Qwen3-4B's prompt 5.2 times faster (477 against 92 tokens/s). A note under the table now says so; "+ GPU" is the GPU's part in the shorter passes.
- **`JANAS_GPU_VERBOSE` puts the calls the GPU does whole on lines of their own** ("gpu alone"): the CPU waits there with nothing to do, and they had been counted as a GPU late, with a share that does not apply to them. Reporting only: the share is learned as before.
- **The hardware page tells what testers found**: on the 780M subgroups of 32 (`JANAS_GPU_SG=32`) cost 14% against the 64 the engine chooses, and on discrete cards the defect of issue #17.


## [2026-10-13] - A discrete GPU's results reach the CPU again

- **On a GPU with memory of its own the products' results reach the CPU again** (issue #17, reported and diagnosed by xpil on a Quadro RTX 5000, with the fix's first form and its test): since the release of 10 October the engine registered its output buffers with the GPU, so that one sharing the host's memory writes them in place, but on a discrete card a registration is a copy in the card's memory, and the CPU went on reading what its buffers held before. Results are written in place only where the GPU shares the host's memory; elsewhere they come back through the staging buffer, as before 10 October. `test_gpu_output_copy` checks it on any GPU (`JANAS_GPU_COPY=1` gives an integrated one the copy path): -12345 instead of 256 before the fix, 256 with it. On that path Qwen3-4B read a prompt in blocks of 64 tokens with the most likely token as read token by token at 0 of 64 positions before the fix (mean difference of the logits 5.73), and at 61 with it (0.098, the fast prompt's usual rounding).


## [2026-10-13] - The GPU's passes of a few tokens

- **Qwen3.8-27B's drafts verified on the GPU a little faster, 7.7 tokens/s in two runs against 7.6 and 7.0** (`JANAS_GPU_SHORT=1`, 64 tokens of code): no chain longer than the GPU's shaders of a few vectors take (a ninth token doubled the pass: 854 ms against 437 for 5), and the recurrent layers' betas and decays read their inputs a quarter as often (3.7 ms a pass of 8 tokens where they took 13.5), with the same results to the bit.
- **Qwen3.8-27B writes 7.6 tokens/s with its drafts verified on the GPU (`JANAS_GPU_SHORT=1`), against 6.3 on the CPU alone** (64 tokens of code, in a series with each build alternated; the figures depend on the length and on the chains the planner chooses): the GPU's products of 2 to 8 vectors on Q5_K and IQ4_XS weights - two thirds of what the model reads - now take the lanes on the vectors too (`q5k_mvd`, `iq4xs_mvd`), a pass of 8 tokens 482.9 ms where it took 509.6, with the same results as the CPU to the bit. On 17408 x 5120, 2 to 8 vectors: Q5_K 1.23-1.38 ms against 1.48-1.54, IQ4_XS 1.85-1.96 against 2.36-2.42.
- **Qwen3.8-27B reads a prompt 19% faster on the GPU, 22.7 tokens/s against 19.0 in blocks of 100 tokens (`janas-bench`, 1,024 tokens: 30.6 against 26.4; llama.cpp 25.6), and with `JANAS_GPU_SHORT=1` writes 8.7 tokens/s with its drafts against 7.0 on the CPU** (128 tokens of code): two of its down matrices are IQ4_NL, in blocks of 32, which had kept every pass of the model off the GPU's single submission; such a layer now takes its activation in both layouts there, as a mixture's shared expert already did. Its logits as close to the reference as before (top-1 197/200, mean |diff| 0.095; the CPU 195/200, 0.098).
- **Qwen3.5-9B with its MTP block writes 24.2 tokens/s with its chains of drafts verified whole on the GPU, against 20.0 on the CPU** (`JANAS_GPU_SHORT=1`, still asked for only): the Gated DeltaNet layers' state is replayed and logged on the GPU as on the CPU, in the same log, so that a pass of drafts goes to the GPU in one submission and the next pass replays there the tokens kept. A short block's betas and decays have a shader of their own, a workgroup a row, where one workgroup took the whole block. A pass of 7.7 tokens: Qwen3.5-0.8B 49.6 to 38.8 ms, 2B 53.7 to 42.4, 9B 161.3 to 132.0. The 9B's logits as close to the reference as the CPU's (top-1 243/256 in blocks of 8, mean |diff| 0.098; the CPU 373/400, 0.099); the text not the CPU's, as the GPU's norms and sums go in their own order.
- **The GPU's products of 2 to 8 vectors on Q4_K weights take 12-25% longer than one vector's, where they took 21-98% longer** (`q4k_mvd`; 17408 x 5120: 0.83, 0.85 and 0.92 ms for 2, 4 and 8 vectors against 0.74 for one): the lanes of a subgroup take the vectors instead of the rows, so that a row's block is read 128 contiguous bytes at a time and its words reach every vector as scalar operands. The shaders before put a row on a lane, and each of their loads touched eight rows: the development laptop's GPU read the weights so at some 50 GB/s against 68. Qwen3-4B with its whole pass on the GPU, ms a token in blocks of 2 / 4 / 8 tokens (a draft chain's verification): 30.0 / 17.2 / 11.1 against 30.9 / 23.0 / 12.7, the same results bit for bit; at 8 tokens the GPU now takes 28% less than the CPU (15.5). On where a subgroup is 8 lanes (Intel); `JANAS_GPU_MVD=0` leaves it out.
- **The same for Q6_K weights** (`q6k_mvd`), whose 210-byte blocks start on a word or two bytes past one: each lane loads the words around its part of the block and shifts them into place. Qwen3-4B's output (151936 x 2560): 7.1 ms for 2 vectors and 7.6 for 8, where it took 10.1 and 16.9, and 9.6 for a single vector. Qwen3-4B with its whole pass on the GPU, now 28.3 / 16.0 / 9.5 ms a token in blocks of 2 / 4 / 8: from 4 tokens up the GPU takes less than the CPU (17.1 at 4, 15.5 at 8), the same results bit for bit.
- **Products of 9 to 63 vectors dispatched with the vectors' groups first**: the groups of the same rows go out together and those after the first find the weights in the L3. 16 vectors of a 17408 x 5120 Q4_K matrix: 1.53 ms instead of 2.06, bit for bit as before.
- **`q4k_mv`, a Q4_K product of a row an invocation with a third fewer instructions**, behind `JANAS_GPU_MV=1` while it is measured: no faster up to 8 vectors, faster beyond (16 vectors 1.22 ms against 1.53).
- **`bench_gemm` puts the weights where the engine has them** (`BENCH_MEM=huge2m`: 2 MB pages; `driver`: the GPU driver's memory). A correction to earlier figures: on a GPU sharing the host's memory the products of a single vector go to the CPU (`JANAS_GPU_MIN_N`), so `bench_gemm`'s "GPU" times for one vector were the CPU's; the GPU's own for 17408 x 5120 is 0.74 ms.
- **Fixed on the way**: the server's store, reading its conversations back from disk with none there, gave `qsort` a null list (harmless in practice, undefined in C; found by the UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer suite).


## [2026-10-12] - janas-bench takes a model by name

- **The GPU's products of a few vectors on IQ4_XS, IQ3_S and IQ4_NL weights a fifth to a third faster**: their table of levels (the IQ3_S grid) read from the workgroup's shared memory instead of a constant array; on 8 vectors of a 17408 x 5120 matrix, IQ4_XS 3.73 to 2.65 ms, IQ3_S 3.57 to 2.67, IQ4_NL 4.35 to 3.46, the results the CPU's to the bit.
